========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it really started after I hurt my ankle. That was the big shift. I mean, I’ve had hard days before, of course, but this whole pattern — waking up with that heaviness, feeling like everything inside me went quiet — it kind of began after the injury.

It’s been about two months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to mark the exact day because at first I was just focused on the ankle and trying to tell myself it was temporary, like, “Okay, rest, heal, get back to it.” But somewhere in there it became this bigger thing, and it’s been going on for around two months.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is this sadness that’s been there when I wake up. It’s heavy, kind of hollow. I don’t know how else to explain it except it feels like the studio lights are on, everything is set up, the space is there, but there’s no music in me anymore. And that’s scary, because movement and music are usually how I know I’m still connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness begin after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started after I hurt my ankle. Yes… I think so. I mean, the ankle injury is upsetting, of course it is — dancing is such a huge part of how I move through the world, not just work but how I feel like myself. So I can understand being frustrated or disappointed.

Like the room is ready, the lights are on, everything should be in place, but there’s no music in me. And that scares me, because it feels deeper than a normal reaction to being hurt.

I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still know what I usually love. I can look at music or think about choreography and some part of me recognizes, “Yes, that’s mine, that matters to me.” But whether I’m feeling pleasure from it in the same way… I’m not sure.

It’s like I haven’t been able to test it normally, because everything is filtered through this injury and being limited. So I don’t want to say I’ve completely lost interest, because that sounds too absolute. I still care. I still notice movement, I still think about phrases and shapes and timing. But the spark feels harder to reach, if that makes sense. Like it’s there somewhere, but muffled.

  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… hopeless is a strong word, but there are mornings where it edges close to that. It’s less like I’m making some clear statement in my head like, “Nothing will ever get better,” and more like I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness already there, before I’ve even done anything.

It feels like… the studio lights are on, everything is technically there, the space is there, my body is there, but there’s no music in me. And when that’s the first thing I feel in the morning, it does make it hard to imagine how I’m supposed to find my way back to feeling like myself. So yes, I guess there is some hopelessness in that, even if I don’t like calling it that.

Not really… not like this. I mean, I’ve had hard times before, obviously — everyone has stretches where they’re stressed or disappointed or thrown off — but this particular whole pattern really seems like it started after I injured my ankle.

So I can understand being frustrated or disappointed.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. It’s not just, “I’m sad because I’m injured.” It’s more like I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness already sitting there before I’ve even had time to think about my ankle. Like the room is ready, the lights are on, everything should be in place, but there’s no music in me. And that scares me, because it feels deeper than a normal reaction to being hurt.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still know what I usually love. I can look at music or think about choreography and some part of me recognizes, “Yes, that’s mine, that matters to me.” But whether I’m feeling pleasure from it in the same way… I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s like I haven’t been able to test it normally, because everything is filtered through this injury and being limited. So I don’t want to say I’ve completely lost interest, because that sounds too absolute. I still care. I still notice movement, I still think about phrases and shapes and timing. But the spark feels harder to reach, if that makes sense. Like it’s there somewhere, but muffled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… hopeless is a strong word, but there are mornings where it edges close to that. It’s less like I’m making some clear statement in my head like, “Nothing will ever get better,” and more like I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness already there, before I’ve even done anything.\n\nIt feels like… the studio lights are on, everything is technically there, the space is there, my body is there, but there’s no music in me. And when that’s the first thing I feel in the morning, it does make it hard to imagine how I’m supposed to find my way back to feeling like myself. So yes, I guess there is some hopelessness in that, even if I don’t like calling it that.\n\nDoctor: Before this ankle injury, have you ever had another period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really… not like this. I mean, I’ve had hard times before, obviously — everyone has stretches where they’re stressed or disappointed or thrown off — but this particular whole pattern really seems like it started after I injured my ankle.\n\nIt’s been going on about two months now, which feels longer when I say it out loud. Yes… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like every single minute is exactly the same, because it isn’t. Yeah, my sleep has been… not steady at all. It’s been all over the place, honestly.

Some nights I don’t fall asleep when I’m supposed to — I’ll end up staying awake sketching formations or moving things around in my head, like if I can just solve some pattern on paper then maybe I’ll feel settled. And then suddenly it’s almost dawn and I realize I haven’t really slept.

Lately it’s changed a little, though. I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. It’s not restful sleep. It feels broken.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is this sadness that’s been there when I wake up. It’s heavy, kind of hollow. And that’s scary, because movement and music are usually how I know I’m still connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness begin after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started after I hurt my ankle. But then the days kept stacking up, and it didn’t just pass in that normal way. So before the injury, no, I don’t think I had a period that felt quite like this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you felt this sadness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. Yeah… I have. It’s not like I made a conscious decision to eat less or anything. At first, when my mind was kind of buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals without even realizing it — like I’d look up and it would be late and I hadn’t had lunch, but it didn’t register as hunger exactly.

I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. Like cardboard, honestly. I’ll make something or someone will put something in front of me, and I can take a few bites, but there’s no pleasure in it. I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” at least not in those exact words.

It’s more that I get very tired of carrying everything. Like I’ll be sitting there and think, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” but then I get scared of even saying that out loud because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it. I don’t have some concrete thing I’m planning or anything like that. It’s more… exhaustion, I guess. A heaviness. A wanting everything to stop feeling so hard.

So, yes and no? Not in a definite way. More like the thought brushes up against me, and then I push it away because I don’t really want to go there.

I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean… I worry, sure, but it doesn’t always feel like ordinary worrying where I can list out categories, like bills, work, health, family, all of that.

It’s more like there’s this background tension sometimes, and then my mind attaches it to whatever is nearby. But if you asked me to name specific things I’m worrying about excessively, I don’t know that I could give you a clean answer. I don’t sit there all day thinking, “What if this happens, what if that happens,” in a neat way.

So… maybe not excessive worrying about lots of separate things. More just feeling unsettled at times, and then trying to figure out what I’m supposed to be worried about, if that makes sense.

So yes, I guess there is some hopelessness in that, even if I don’t like calling it that.\n\nDoctor: Before this ankle injury, have you ever had another period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really… not like this. At first I thought it was just the immediate shock of the injury, like, okay, of course I’m upset, of course I feel off. But then the days kept stacking up, and it didn’t just pass in that normal way. So before the injury, no, I don’t think I had a period that felt quite like this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you felt this sadness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like every single minute is exactly the same, because it isn’t. Sometimes I can get distracted or I can talk myself into functioning for a while. But when I really look at the pattern, most mornings I wake up with that same heavy, hollow sadness already there.\n\nIt’s like before I even have a chance to decide what kind of day it’s going to be, it’s waiting for me. And that’s honestly a big part of what brings me in — that feeling like the studio lights are on, the room is there, I’m there, but there’s no music in me anymore. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has been… not steady at all. It’s been all over the place, honestly.\n\nSome nights I don’t fall asleep when I’m supposed to — I’ll end up staying awake sketching formations or moving things around in my head, like if I can just solve some pattern on paper then maybe I’ll feel settled. And then suddenly it’s almost dawn and I realize I haven’t really slept.\n\nLately it’s changed a little, though. I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. It’s not restful sleep. It feels broken.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I made a conscious decision to eat less or anything. At first, when my mind was kind of buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals without even realizing it — like I’d look up and it would be late and I hadn’t had lunch, but it didn’t register as hunger exactly.\n\nAnd now it’s more like… food is there, but it tastes flat. Like cardboard, honestly. I’ll make something or someone will put something in front of me, and I can take a few bites, but there’s no pleasure in it. It feels mechanical.\n\nI’ve noticed my rehearsal clothes hanging looser than usual, especially around my waist and hips. As a dancer you notice those little changes in how fabric sits on your body, even if you’re trying not to obsess over it. So yes, my appetite has definitely dropped.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” at least not in those exact words.\n\nIt’s more that I get very tired of carrying everything. Like I’ll be sitting there and think, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” but then I get scared of even saying that out loud because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it. I don’t have some concrete thing I’m planning or anything like that. It’s more… exhaustion, I guess. A heaviness. A wanting everything to stop feeling so hard.\n\nSo, yes and no? Not in a definite way. More like the thought brushes up against me, and then I push it away because I don’t really want to go there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean… I worry, sure, but it doesn’t always feel like ordinary worrying where I can list out categories, like bills, work, health, family, all of that.\n\nIt’s more like there’s this background tension sometimes, and then my mind attaches it to whatever is nearby. But if you asked me to name specific things I’m worrying about excessively, I don’t know that I could give you a clean answer. I don’t sit there all day thinking, “What if this happens, what if that happens,” in a neat way.\n\nSo… maybe not excessive worrying about lots of separate things. More just feeling unsettled at times, and then trying to figure out what I’m supposed to be worried about, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes… I think so. It’s strange because I’m so used to my body being, you know, responsive. In the studio, if I want to cross the room to grab my notebook or mark something down, normally I just do it without thinking — my body kind of organizes itself around the music or the space.

But recently even something that small feels slowed down. Like crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like I’m moving through wet sand instead of moving through music. My legs go, but there’s this drag to everything, like the signal from my brain to my body is taking too long.

I don’t know if other people notice it as much, but I notice it because my whole life is built around movement. Even my speech sometimes feels like I’m having to pull the words up from somewhere deeper than usual. Not always, but enough that I’m aware of it.

And that’s scary, because movement and music are usually how I know I’m still connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness begin after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started after I hurt my ankle. That was the big shift. I mean, I’ve had hard days before, of course, but this whole pattern — waking up with that heaviness, feeling like everything inside me went quiet — it kind of began after the injury.\n\nIt’s been about two months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to mark the exact day because at first I was just focused on the ankle and trying to tell myself it was temporary, like, “Okay, rest, heal, get back to it.” But somewhere in there it became this bigger thing, and it’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect just from being injured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, the ankle injury is upsetting, of course it is — dancing is such a huge part of how I move through the world, not just work but how I feel like myself. So I can understand being frustrated or disappointed.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. It’s not just, “I’m sad because I’m injured.” It’s more like I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness already sitting there before I’ve even had time to think about my ankle. Like the room is ready, the lights are on, everything should be in place, but there’s no music in me. And that scares me, because it feels deeper than a normal reaction to being hurt.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still know what I usually love. I can look at music or think about choreography and some part of me recognizes, “Yes, that’s mine, that matters to me.” But whether I’m feeling pleasure from it in the same way… I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s like I haven’t been able to test it normally, because everything is filtered through this injury and being limited. So I don’t want to say I’ve completely lost interest, because that sounds too absolute. I still care. I still notice movement, I still think about phrases and shapes and timing. But the spark feels harder to reach, if that makes sense. Like it’s there somewhere, but muffled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… hopeless is a strong word, but there are mornings where it edges close to that. It’s less like I’m making some clear statement in my head like, “Nothing will ever get better,” and more like I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness already there, before I’ve even done anything.\n\nIt feels like… the studio lights are on, everything is technically there, the space is there, my body is there, but there’s no music in me. And when that’s the first thing I feel in the morning, it does make it hard to imagine how I’m supposed to find my way back to feeling like myself. So yes, I guess there is some hopelessness in that, even if I don’t like calling it that.\n\nDoctor: Before this ankle injury, have you ever had another period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really… not like this. I mean, I’ve had hard times before, obviously — everyone has stretches where they’re stressed or disappointed or thrown off — but this particular whole pattern really seems like it started after I injured my ankle.\n\nIt’s been going on about two months now, which feels longer when I say it out loud. At first I thought it was just the immediate shock of the injury, like, okay, of course I’m upset, of course I feel off. But then the days kept stacking up, and it didn’t just pass in that normal way. So before the injury, no, I don’t think I had a period that felt quite like this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you felt this sadness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like every single minute is exactly the same, because it isn’t. Sometimes I can get distracted or I can talk myself into functioning for a while. But when I really look at the pattern, most mornings I wake up with that same heavy, hollow sadness already there.\n\nIt’s like before I even have a chance to decide what kind of day it’s going to be, it’s waiting for me. And that’s honestly a big part of what brings me in — that feeling like the studio lights are on, the room is there, I’m there, but there’s no music in me anymore. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has been… not steady at all. It’s been all over the place, honestly.\n\nSome nights I don’t fall asleep when I’m supposed to — I’ll end up staying awake sketching formations or moving things around in my head, like if I can just solve some pattern on paper then maybe I’ll feel settled. And then suddenly it’s almost dawn and I realize I haven’t really slept.\n\nLately it’s changed a little, though. I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. It’s not restful sleep. It feels broken.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I made a conscious decision to eat less or anything. At first, when my mind was kind of buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals without even realizing it — like I’d look up and it would be late and I hadn’t had lunch, but it didn’t register as hunger exactly.\n\nAnd now it’s more like… food is there, but it tastes flat. Like cardboard, honestly. I’ll make something or someone will put something in front of me, and I can take a few bites, but there’s no pleasure in it. It feels mechanical.\n\nI’ve noticed my rehearsal clothes hanging looser than usual, especially around my waist and hips. As a dancer you notice those little changes in how fabric sits on your body, even if you’re trying not to obsess over it. So yes, my appetite has definitely dropped.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” at least not in those exact words.\n\nIt’s more that I get very tired of carrying everything. Like I’ll be sitting there and think, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” but then I get scared of even saying that out loud because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it. I don’t have some concrete thing I’m planning or anything like that. It’s more… exhaustion, I guess. A heaviness. A wanting everything to stop feeling so hard.\n\nSo, yes and no? More just feeling unsettled at times, and then trying to figure out what I’m supposed to be worried about, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. [

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” at least not in those exact words.\n\nIt’s more that I get very tired of carrying everything. Like I’ll be sitting there and think, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” but then I get scared of even saying that out loud because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it. I don’t have some concrete thing I’m planning or anything like that. It’s more… exhaustion, I guess. A heaviness. A wanting everything to stop feeling so hard.\n\nSo, yes and no? Not in a definite way. More like the thought brushes up against me, and then I push it away because I don’t really want to go there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” exactly. I mean… I worry, sure, but it doesn’t always feel like ordinary worrying where I can list out categories, like bills, work, health, family, all of that.\n\nIt’s more like there’s this background tension sometimes, and then my mind attaches it to whatever is nearby. But if you asked me to name specific things I’m worrying about excessively, I don’t know that I could give you a clean answer. I don’t sit there all day thinking, “What if this happens, what if that happens,” in a neat way.\n\nSo… maybe not excessive worrying about lots of separate things. More just feeling unsettled at times, and then trying to figure out what I’m supposed to be worried about, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s strange because I’m so used to my body being, you know, responsive. In the studio, if I want to cross the room to grab my notebook or mark something down, normally I just do it without thinking — my body kind of organizes itself around the music or the space.\n\nBut recently even something that small feels slowed down. Like crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like I’m moving through wet sand instead of moving through music. My legs go, but there’s this drag to everything, like the signal from my brain to my body is taking too long.\n\nI don’t know if other people notice it as much, but I notice it because my whole life is built around movement. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually the part that scares me a little, because I can understand being sad when I’m thinking about my ankle, or about not dancing the way I’m used to. That makes sense to me. I can trace that.

But this feels… separate from that sometimes. Like I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even had a full thought. Just this heavy, hollow sadness sitting in my chest, and I haven’t even remembered the day yet, if that makes sense. It’s like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. Everything is technically there — the room, my body, the day — but something that usually makes it feel alive is missing.

And then, yes, my mind may eventually go to the ankle or to dancing because those are the obvious explanations, but the sadness isn’t always waiting for a reason. Sometimes it’s just there first, and then I start trying to explain it afterward.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is this sadness that’s been there when I wake up. It’s heavy, kind of hollow. I don’t know how else to explain it except it feels like the studio lights are on, everything is set up, the space is there, but there’s no music in me anymore. And that’s scary, because movement and music are usually how I know I’m still connected to myself.\n\nDoctor: Did this sadness begin after a specific stressful event or major change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it really started after I hurt my ankle. That was the big shift. I mean, I’ve had hard days before, of course, but this whole pattern — waking up with that heaviness, feeling like everything inside me went quiet — it kind of began after the injury.\n\nIt’s been about two months now, I think. Maybe a little hard to mark the exact day because at first I was just focused on the ankle and trying to tell myself it was temporary, like, “Okay, rest, heal, get back to it.” But somewhere in there it became this bigger thing, and it’s been going on for around two months.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem stronger than you would expect just from being injured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, the ankle injury is upsetting, of course it is — dancing is such a huge part of how I move through the world, not just work but how I feel like myself. So I can understand being frustrated or disappointed.\n\nBut this feels… bigger than that. It’s not just, “I’m sad because I’m injured.” It’s more like I wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow sadness already sitting there before I’ve even had time to think about my ankle. Like the room is ready, the lights are on, everything should be in place, but there’s no music in me. And that scares me, because it feels deeper than a normal reaction to being hurt.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still know what I usually love. I can look at music or think about choreography and some part of me recognizes, “Yes, that’s mine, that matters to me.” But whether I’m feeling pleasure from it in the same way… I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s like I haven’t been able to test it normally, because everything is filtered through this injury and being limited. So I don’t want to say I’ve completely lost interest, because that sounds too absolute. I still care. I still notice movement, I still think about phrases and shapes and timing. But the spark feels harder to reach, if that makes sense. Like it’s there somewhere, but muffled.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yeah… hopeless is a strong word, but there are mornings where it edges close to that. It’s less like I’m making some clear statement in my head like, “Nothing will ever get better,” and more like I wake up with this heavy, hollow sadness already there, before I’ve even done anything.\n\nIt feels like… the studio lights are on, everything is technically there, the space is there, my body is there, but there’s no music in me. And when that’s the first thing I feel in the morning, it does make it hard to imagine how I’m supposed to find my way back to feeling like myself. So yes, I guess there is some hopelessness in that, even if I don’t like calling it that.\n\nDoctor: Before this ankle injury, have you ever had another period like this in your life?\n\nPatient: Not really… not like this. I mean, I’ve had hard times before, obviously — everyone has stretches where they’re stressed or disappointed or thrown off — but this particular whole pattern really seems like it started after I injured my ankle.\n\nIt’s been going on about two months now, which feels longer when I say it out loud. At first I thought it was just the immediate shock of the injury, like, okay, of course I’m upset, of course I feel off. But then the days kept stacking up, and it didn’t just pass in that normal way. So before the injury, no, I don’t think I had a period that felt quite like this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two months, have you felt this sadness nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. I don’t want to make it sound like every single minute is exactly the same, because it isn’t. Sometimes I can get distracted or I can talk myself into functioning for a while. But when I really look at the pattern, most mornings I wake up with that same heavy, hollow sadness already there.\n\nIt’s like before I even have a chance to decide what kind of day it’s going to be, it’s waiting for me. And that’s honestly a big part of what brings me in — that feeling like the studio lights are on, the room is there, I’m there, but there’s no music in me anymore. It’s been hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my sleep has been… not steady at all. It’s been all over the place, honestly.\n\nSome nights I don’t fall asleep when I’m supposed to — I’ll end up staying awake sketching formations or moving things around in my head, like if I can just solve some pattern on paper then maybe I’ll feel settled. And then suddenly it’s almost dawn and I realize I haven’t really slept.\n\nLately it’s changed a little, though. I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. It’s not restful sleep. It feels broken.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I made a conscious decision to eat less or anything. At first, when my mind was kind of buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals without even realizing it — like I’d look up and it would be late and I hadn’t had lunch, but it didn’t register as hunger exactly.\n\nAnd now it’s more like… food is there, but it tastes flat. Like cardboard, honestly. I’ll make something or someone will put something in front of me, and I can take a few bites, but there’s no pleasure in it. It feels mechanical.\n\nI’ve noticed my rehearsal clothes hanging looser than usual, especially around my waist and hips. As a dancer you notice those little changes in how fabric sits on your body, even if you’re trying not to obsess over it. So yes, my appetite has definitely dropped.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know exactly how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I have a clear thought like, “life isn’t worth living,” at least not in those exact words.\n\nIt’s more that I get very tired of carrying everything. Like I’ll be sitting there and think, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” but then I get scared of even saying that out loud because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it. I don’t have some concrete thing I’m planning or anything like that. It’s more… exhaustion, I guess. A heaviness. A wanting everything to stop feeling so hard.\n\nSo, yes and no? Like crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like I’m moving through wet sand instead of moving through music. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is out of proportion. Or at least it feels different from what I would normally expect.

I mean, I know an ankle injury would upset me. Dance is such a huge part of how I understand myself and my days, so of course I’d be sad or frustrated about that. I don’t want to pretend the injury doesn’t matter. It does.

But what brings me in is more than that. I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness already sitting there, before I’ve even really thought about my ankle or what I can’t do. It’s like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And that feels bigger, or deeper, than just being disappointed about being injured.

If I were just sad when I looked at my ankle or tried to move and couldn’t, I could understand that. But this feels like it fills the room before anything has even happened. So… yes, I think it’s more than I’d normally expect.

It’s not restful sleep. It feels broken.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a drop in your appetite?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s not like I made a conscious decision to eat less or anything. I’ll be so exhausted that I do fall asleep, but then I’m awake at four or five in the morning with my stomach tight, like my body is already bracing for the day before I’ve even opened my eyes properly. Like cardboard, honestly. I’ll make something or someone will put something in front of me, and I can take a few bites, but there’s no pleasure in it. It feels mechanical.\n\nI’ve noticed my rehearsal clothes hanging looser than usual, especially around my waist and hips. As a dancer you notice those little changes in how fabric sits on your body, even if you’re trying not to obsess over it. Like I’ll be sitting there and think, “I don’t know how I’m supposed to keep doing this,” but then I get scared of even saying that out loud because it sounds more dramatic than I mean it. I don’t have some concrete thing I’m planning or anything like that. It’s more… exhaustion, I guess. A heaviness. A wanting everything to stop feeling so hard.\n\nSo, yes and no? In the studio, if I want to cross the room to grab my notebook or mark something down, normally I just do it without thinking — my body kind of organizes itself around the music or the space.\n\nBut recently even something that small feels slowed down. Like crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels like I’m moving through wet sand instead of moving through music. My legs go, but there’s this drag to everything, like the signal from my brain to my body is taking too long.\n\nI don’t know if other people notice it as much, but I notice it because my whole life is built around movement. Even my speech sometimes feels like I’m having to pull the words up from somewhere deeper than usual. Not always, but enough that I’m aware of it.\n\nDoctor: When the sadness comes, is it there even at times when you’re not thinking about your ankle or being unable to dance?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually the part that scares me a little, because I can understand being sad when I’m thinking about my ankle, or about not dancing the way I’m used to. That makes sense to me. I can trace that.\n\nBut this feels… separate from that sometimes. Like I wake up and it’s already there before I’ve even had a full thought. Just this heavy, hollow sadness sitting in my chest, and I haven’t even remembered the day yet, if that makes sense. It’s like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. Everything is technically there — the room, my body, the day — but something that usually makes it feel alive is missing.\n\nAnd then, yes, my mind may eventually go to the ankle or to dancing because those are the obvious explanations, but the sadness isn’t always waiting for a reason. Sometimes it’s just there first, and then I start trying to explain it afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness has been out of proportion to what you would normally expect after an ankle injury?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is out of proportion. Or at least it feels different from what I would normally expect.\n\nI mean, I know an ankle injury would upset me. Dance is such a huge part of how I understand myself and my days, so of course I’d be sad or frustrated about that. I don’t want to pretend the injury doesn’t matter. It does.\n\nBut what brings me in is more than that. I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness already sitting there, before I’ve even really thought about my ankle or what I can’t do. It’s like the studio lights are on but there’s no music in me anymore. And that feels bigger, or deeper, than just being disappointed about being injured.\n\nIf I were just sad when I looked at my ankle or tried to move and couldn’t, I could understand that. But this feels like it fills the room before anything has even happened. So… yes, I think it’s more than I’d normally expect."
